========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, hello, Doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I just didn't know where else to go, to be perfectly honest with you. I've been putting this off for a long time, but I finally realized I needed help because... well, it's these nights I've been having. These awful nights of just eating. 

I came in today because after it happens, I just sit there at my kitchen table, and it's so quiet, and I look around... and there are just crumbs everywhere. Crumbs all over my lap, all over the floor, and all these empty containers and wrappers just scattered all around me. And I just sit in the middle of it all, and this feeling washes over me... I feel so incredibly ashamed. Just completely and utterly disgusted with myself. I'm sixty-five years old, and I look at the mess and think, how on earth did I end up here again? 

It’s such a dark, heavy feeling, Doctor. It gets so bad that I can hardly even bring myself to pray afterward. Faith has always been my rock, but I just feel too unworthy or too embarrassed to even speak to the Lord when I'm sitting there in the middle of all that trash. And then the next morning, especially if it's a Sunday, I go to service and I can hardly even look folks at church in the eye. I just keep my head down, feeling like everybody must be able to see right through me, like they know what a terrible, out-of-control mess I am behind closed doors. I just... I can't keep living with this kind of shame, I really can't.

Oh, heavens no. No, I don't do anything like that. I know... I know you hear about people doing that, young girls especially, making themselves sick on purpose, but I just could never bring myself to do it. The very thought of throwing up just terrifies me, it really does. I haven't thrown up since I had a terrible bout of the stomach flu back in, oh, it must have been 2015, and it was just awful. 

And laxatives, well, my digestion is already so sensitive at my age, I wouldn't dare take anything like that to clear myself out. I bought some milk of magnesia once years ago, but I never, ever used it for this. I'd be too scared of what it would do to my system. 

And as for exercise... well, Doctor, look at me. I'm sixty-five years old and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be. The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. 

So no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. 

And that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. 

And the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. 

Tuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.

It gets so bad that I can hardly even bring myself to pray afterward. Faith has always been my rock, but I just feel too unworthy or too embarrassed to even speak to the Lord when I'm sitting there in the middle of all that trash. And then the next morning, especially if it's a Sunday, I go to service and I can hardly even look folks at church in the eye. I just keep my head down, feeling like everybody must be able to see right through me, like they know what a terrible, out-of-control mess I am behind closed doors. I just... I can't keep living with this kind of shame, I really can't.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, I don't do anything like that. I know... I know you hear about people doing that, young girls especially, making themselves sick on purpose, but I just could never bring myself to do it. The very thought of throwing up just terrifies me, it really does. I haven't thrown up since I had a terrible bout of the stomach flu back in, oh, it must have been 2015, and it was just awful. \n\nAnd laxatives, well, my digestion is already so sensitive at my age, I wouldn't dare take anything like that to clear myself out. I bought some milk of magnesia once years ago, but I never, ever used it for this. I'd be too scared of what it would do to my system. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, Doctor, look at me. I'm sixty-five years old and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be. The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. \n\nSo no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. 

I'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. 

But it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.

Faith has always been my rock, but I just feel too unworthy or too embarrassed to even speak to the Lord when I'm sitting there in the middle of all that trash. And then the next morning, especially if it's a Sunday, I go to service and I can hardly even look folks at church in the eye. I just keep my head down, feeling like everybody must be able to see right through me, like they know what a terrible, out-of-control mess I am behind closed doors. I just... I can't keep living with this kind of shame, I really can't.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, I don't do anything like that. I know... I know you hear about people doing that, young girls especially, making themselves sick on purpose, but I just could never bring myself to do it. The very thought of throwing up just terrifies me, it really does. I haven't thrown up since I had a terrible bout of the stomach flu back in, oh, it must have been 2015, and it was just awful. \n\nAnd laxatives, well, my digestion is already so sensitive at my age, I wouldn't dare take anything like that to clear myself out. I bought some milk of magnesia once years ago, but I never, ever used it for this. I'd be too scared of what it would do to my system. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, Doctor, look at me. I'm sixty-five years old and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be. The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. \n\nSo no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. \n\nI'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. 

When I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. So the shape of my body, the number on the scale... that's not what keeps me up at night. It's not what breaks my heart when I'm lying there in the dark after one of my... spells. 

What distresses me, what really just tears me apart inside, isn't how I look in my clothes the next day. It's the absolute lack of control. It's the feeling that I've let myself down again. It's the fact that I can't seem to stop my own hands from putting food into my mouth, even when my brain is screaming at me to stop. That's what upsets me so terribly. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. That's the part that hurts. Not the mirror. Just... the knowing what I've done.

manifestation: At bridge or church suppers I make a point of taking a modest little plate, then later after everyone has gone home I pull the leftovers out and eat in the kitchen where nobody can see me.\n- key: S055\n  name: Negative_Affect_After_Binge\n  description: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n  manifestation: I came because after these nights of eating I sit there with crumbs and empty containers around me feeling so ashamed and disgusted with myself that I can hardly pray or look folks at church in the eye the next morning.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: The binge eating occurs, on average, at least once a week for 3 months\n  manifestation: Since my friend passed, these late-night eating spells have been happening at least once or twice most weeks, and there has not been a month where they really let up.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  manifestation: I have started turning down some bridge nights and church kitchen duties because I am so worried someone will notice the missing leftovers or ask why I seem ashamed.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 5 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about five months, ever since my closest bridge partner and church friend died.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S055\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. \n\nI'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. So the shape of my body, the number on the scale... that's not what keeps me up at night. It's not what breaks my heart when I'm lying there in the dark after one of my... spells. \n\nWhat distresses me, what really just tears me apart inside, isn't how I look in my clothes the next day. It's the absolute lack of control. It's the feeling that I've let myself down again. It's the fact that I can't seem to stop my own hands from putting food into my mouth, even when my brain is screaming at me to stop. That's what upsets me so terribly. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. That's the part that hurts. Not the mirror. Just... the knowing what I've done.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo these late-night eating episodes usually happen when you’re feeling particularly sad, lonely, or upset, like after our conversation about your friend passing away?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, you know... I've thought about that a lot, Doctor. I really have. Because it would make so much sense, wouldn't it? If I could just say, 'Oh, I was feeling so blue about Mary passing, and that's why I ate all those cookies.' But... well, it's not quite as simple as that. I mean, yes, the whole thing started after she passed, like I told you, and I certainly miss her every single day. I really do. But these days, when I find myself standing in the pantry... I'm not always crying. I'm not always feeling particularly sad or even thinking about her in that exact moment. 

Sometimes I'm just... bored, maybe? Or just feeling a little restless. Like last Tuesday, for instance. I had a perfectly fine day. I went to the grocery store, I talked to my niece on the telephone—she's doing so well in her new job, by the way—and I even watched a lovely program on the television about gardens in England. I wasn't upset at all. I was actually feeling quite content. But then eight o'clock rolled around, and the house got quiet, and... well, there I was again. 

So I can't honestly sit here and tell you that I only do it when I'm feeling down in the dumps. It's almost like it's become a habit now, a terrible, terrible habit. It's like my body just expects it, regardless of what my mood is doing. I mean, sure, if I've had a rough day or if I'm feeling particularly lonely, it might trigger it, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just when I'm sad or upset. It happens when I'm perfectly fine, too. And I think that's part of what makes it so confusing to me. If it was just grief, I feel like I could understand it better. But this... this just feels like something else entirely.

I've been putting this off for a long time, but I finally realized I needed help because... well, it's these nights I've been having. These awful nights of just eating. \n\nI came in today because after it happens, I just sit there at my kitchen table, and it's so quiet, and I look around... and there are just crumbs everywhere. Crumbs all over my lap, all over the floor, and all these empty containers and wrappers just scattered all around me. And I just sit in the middle of it all, and this feeling washes over me... I feel so incredibly ashamed. Just completely and utterly disgusted with myself. I'm sixty-five years old, and I look at the mess and think, how on earth did I end up here again? \n\nIt’s such a dark, heavy feeling, Doctor. It gets so bad that I can hardly even bring myself to pray afterward. Faith has always been my rock, but I just feel too unworthy or too embarrassed to even speak to the Lord when I'm sitting there in the middle of all that trash. And then the next morning, especially if it's a Sunday, I go to service and I can hardly even look folks at church in the eye. I just keep my head down, feeling like everybody must be able to see right through me, like they know what a terrible, out-of-control mess I am behind closed doors. I just... I can't keep living with this kind of shame, I really can't.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, I don't do anything like that. I know... I know you hear about people doing that, young girls especially, making themselves sick on purpose, but I just could never bring myself to do it. The very thought of throwing up just terrifies me, it really does. I haven't thrown up since I had a terrible bout of the stomach flu back in, oh, it must have been 2015, and it was just awful. \n\nAnd laxatives, well, my digestion is already so sensitive at my age, I wouldn't dare take anything like that to clear myself out. I bought some milk of magnesia once years ago, but I never, ever used it for this. I'd be too scared of what it would do to my system. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, Doctor, look at me. I'm sixty-five years old and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be. The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. \n\nSo no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. \n\nI'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. So the shape of my body, the number on the scale... that's not what keeps me up at night. It's not what breaks my heart when I'm lying there in the dark after one of my... spells. \n\nWhat distresses me, what really just tears me apart inside, isn't how I look in my clothes the next day. It's the absolute lack of control. It's the feeling that I've let myself down again. It's the fact that I can't seem to stop my own hands from putting food into my mouth, even when my brain is screaming at me to stop. That's what upsets me so terribly. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. That's the part that hurts. Not the mirror. Just... the knowing what I've done.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes usually happen when you’re feeling particularly sad, lonely, or upset, like after our conversation about your friend passing away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know... I've thought about that a lot, Doctor. I really have. Because it would make so much sense, wouldn't it? If I could just say, 'Oh, I was feeling so blue about Mary passing, and that's why I ate all those cookies.' But... well, it's not quite as simple as that. I mean, yes, the whole thing started after she passed, like I told you, and I certainly miss her every single day. I really do. But these days, when I find myself standing in the pantry... I'm not always crying. I'm not always feeling particularly sad or even thinking about her in that exact moment. \n\nSometimes I'm just... bored, maybe? Or just feeling a little restless. Like last Tuesday, for instance. I had a perfectly fine day. I went to the grocery store, I talked to my niece on the telephone—she's doing so well in her new job, by the way—and I even watched a lovely program on the television about gardens in England. I wasn't upset at all. I was actually feeling quite content. But then eight o'clock rolled around, and the house got quiet, and... well, there I was again. \n\nSo I can't honestly sit here and tell you that I only do it when I'm feeling down in the dumps. It's almost like it's become a habit now, a terrible, terrible habit. It's like my body just expects it, regardless of what my mood is doing. I mean, sure, if I've had a rough day or if I'm feeling particularly lonely, it might trigger it, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just when I'm sad or upset. It happens when I'm perfectly fine, too. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, my heavens, yes. It's... well, it's almost like a race, Doctor, and I don't even know who in the world I'm racing against. I don't even take the time to sit down at the little dinette table. You'd think a person my age would put a portion on a proper plate, maybe get a napkin, and sit down like a civilized human being, but no. I am just standing right there at the kitchen counter. 

And the speed... well, it's just shameful, really. I eat so fast standing at the kitchen counter that I barely even taste what I'm putting in my mouth. It could be a nice piece of pound cake—the kind with the little lemon glaze I sometimes pick up from the bakery, which usually I'd savor with a cup of decaf—or it could just be those dry crackers I was telling you about. It doesn't even matter which it is, because I'm not enjoying it. I'm just rushing it all down. 

I actually... well, sometimes I catch myself swallowing before I've rightly chewed. My mother, rest her soul, she used to always tell us girls to chew our food twenty times before swallowing. For proper digestion, she'd say, and it's just good manners. But when I'm standing there in the quiet... all that goes right out the window. It's just bite after bite, so terribly fast, like I'm afraid someone is going to march right through the front door and snatch the box away from me. And I live completely alone! There is nobody here to take it from me. But I still practically inhale that pound cake, not savoring a single crumb of it, just rushing, rushing, rushing. In the moment, I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and breathe.

The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. \n\nSo no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. \n\nI'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. So the shape of my body, the number on the scale... that's not what keeps me up at night. It's not what breaks my heart when I'm lying there in the dark after one of my... spells. \n\nWhat distresses me, what really just tears me apart inside, isn't how I look in my clothes the next day. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. That's the part that hurts. Not the mirror. Just... the knowing what I've done.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes usually happen when you’re feeling particularly sad, lonely, or upset, like after our conversation about your friend passing away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know... Female / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S055\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. I've thought about that a lot, Doctor. I really have. Because it would make so much sense, wouldn't it? I mean, yes, the whole thing started after she passed, like I told you, and I certainly miss her every single day. I really do. But these days, when I find myself standing in the pantry... I'm not always crying. I'm not always feeling particularly sad or even thinking about her in that exact moment. \n\nSometimes I'm just... bored, maybe? Or just feeling a little restless. I went to the grocery store, I talked to my niece on the telephone—she's doing so well in her new job, by the way—and I even watched a lovely program on the television about gardens in England. I wasn't upset at all. I was actually feeling quite content. But then eight o'clock rolled around, and the house got quiet, and... well, there I was again. \n\nSo I can't honestly sit here and tell you that I only do it when I'm feeling down in the dumps. It's... well, it's almost like a race, Doctor, and I don't even know who in the world I'm racing against. I don't even take the time to sit down at the little dinette table. You'd think a person my age would put a portion on a proper plate, maybe get a napkin, and sit down like a civilized human being, but no. I am just standing right there at the kitchen counter. \n\nAnd the speed... well, it's just shameful, really. I eat so fast standing at the kitchen counter that I barely even taste what I'm putting in my mouth. It doesn't even matter which it is, because I'm not enjoying it. I'm just rushing it all down. \n\nI actually... well, sometimes I catch myself swallowing before I've rightly chewed. In the moment, I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and breathe.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're in the middle of one of these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you've completely lost control over your eating, like you can't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, absolutely. That is exactly it, Doctor. It's the most terrifying feeling in the world, to be perfectly honest with you. You know, I've always prided myself on being a very sensible woman. I managed a household for decades, I kept everything running smoothly, I always made sure the bills were paid on time... I was always the one steering the ship, so to speak. But this... this is entirely different. 

It's like... well, once I take that very first bite after the house is quiet—you know, when the television is off and it's just me and the hum of the refrigerator—it's like all my good sense just steps completely aside. It just packs its bags and leaves the room. And the strangest, most awful part is that my mind is still working. I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, and I'm standing there literally telling myself, "Belinda, stop now. You don't need this, Belinda, just put the wrapper in the trash and go to bed." I plead with myself. I really do. 

But it doesn't do a lick of good. It's like there's a total disconnect between my brain and my body. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hands just keep reaching for more. They just keep going right back into the package, over and over, like they have a mind of their own. I'll just watch my own hand reach out, grab another piece, and bring it to my mouth, and I can't seem to halt the motion no matter how hard I try. It's like being a passenger in my own body, Doctor. I just... I completely lose the reins. And I don't know how to get them back once that first bite happens. I just can't stop it.

It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. I eat so fast standing at the kitchen counter that I barely even taste what I'm putting in my mouth. It could be a nice piece of pound cake—the kind with the little lemon glaze I sometimes pick up from the bakery, which usually I'd savor with a cup of decaf—or it could just be those dry crackers I was telling you about. It doesn't even matter which it is, because I'm not enjoying it. I'm just rushing it all down. \n\nI actually... well, sometimes I catch myself swallowing before I've rightly chewed. My mother, rest her soul, she used to always tell us girls to chew our food twenty times before swallowing. For proper digestion, she'd say, and it's just good manners. But when I'm standing there in the quiet... all that goes right out the window. It's just bite after bite, so terribly fast, like I'm afraid someone is going to march right through the front door and snatch the box away from me. And I live completely alone! There is nobody here to take it from me. But I still practically inhale that pound cake, not savoring a single crumb of it, just rushing, rushing, rushing. In the moment, I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and breathe.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of one of these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you've completely lost control over your eating, like you can't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. That is exactly it, Doctor. It's the most terrifying feeling in the world, to be perfectly honest with you. You know, I've always prided myself on being a very sensible woman. I managed a household for decades, I kept everything running smoothly, I always made sure the bills were paid on time... I was always the one steering the ship, so to speak. But this... this is entirely different. \n\nIt's like... well, once I take that very first bite after the house is quiet—you know, when the television is off and it's just me and the hum of the refrigerator—it's like all my good sense just steps completely aside. It just packs its bags and leaves the room. And the strangest, most awful part is that my mind is still working. I'm fully aware of what I'm doing, and I'm standing there literally telling myself, \"Belinda, stop now. You don't need this, Belinda, just put the wrapper in the trash and go to bed.\" I plead with myself. I really do. \n\nBut it doesn't do a lick of good. It's like there's a total disconnect between my brain and my body. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hands just keep reaching for more. They just keep going right back into the package, over and over, like they have a mind of their own. I'll just watch my own hand reach out, grab another piece, and bring it to my mouth, and I can't seem to halt the motion no matter how hard I try. It's like being a passenger in my own body, Doctor. I just... I completely lose the reins. And I don't know how to get them back once that first bite happens. I just can't stop it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you usually eat these late-night episodes even when you're not actually hungry?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 65 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hungry? Well, goodness, let me think about that for a moment, Doctor. Because... I'm really just not sure. I mean, you would think a woman my age would know when her own stomach is empty, wouldn't you? It sounds so silly to even say it out loud, that I don't know if I'm hungry or not. But I really haven't noticed. I haven't paid a lick of attention to what my stomach is actually doing during all of this. 

You see, back when my husband was still with us, we had our supper at exactly five-thirty every single evening. You could set a watch by it. And by five o'clock, I'd feel that familiar little rumble in my tummy, just a little grumble letting me know it was time to put the potatoes on to boil or get the meatloaf out of the oven. That was real, physical hunger. I knew exactly what that felt like. But nowadays, with these late-night... situations... I just couldn't tell you. 

I mean, I usually have my supper around six o'clock now, just a small plate, maybe some baked chicken and a nice green bean. So by eight or nine o'clock, when the house gets so terribly quiet and I find myself migrating back into the kitchen, I shouldn't technically be hungry, should I? But when I'm standing there, tearing into a package of something, I'm not stopping to ask myself, "Belinda, is your stomach growling?" I'm just entirely caught up in the rush of it all, like I was saying before. It's more of a... a feeling in my head, or maybe my hands, rather than a rumbling in my belly. 

But as for actual, physical hunger? Like the kind where you genuinely need nourishment? I honestly haven't the faintest idea. It all happens in such a frantic blur that I just haven't stopped to check in with my body to see if I'm actually hungry or not. I'm sorry, I wish I could give you a more helpful answer than that, but I just really haven't noticed one way or the other.

I'm Dr. [Name]. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, hello, Doctor. Thank you for fitting me in. I, um... I just didn't know where else to go, to be perfectly honest with you. I've been putting this off for a long time, but I finally realized I needed help because... well, it's these nights I've been having. These awful nights of just eating. \n\nI came in today because after it happens, I just sit there at my kitchen table, and it's so quiet, and I look around... and there are just crumbs everywhere. Crumbs all over my lap, all over the floor, and all these empty containers and wrappers just scattered all around me. And I just sit in the middle of it all, and this feeling washes over me... I feel so incredibly ashamed. Just completely and utterly disgusted with myself. I'm sixty-five years old, and I look at the mess and think, how on earth did I end up here again? \n\nIt’s such a dark, heavy feeling, Doctor. It gets so bad that I can hardly even bring myself to pray afterward. Faith has always been my rock, but I just feel too unworthy or too embarrassed to even speak to the Lord when I'm sitting there in the middle of all that trash. And then the next morning, especially if it's a Sunday, I go to service and I can hardly even look folks at church in the eye. I just keep my head down, feeling like everybody must be able to see right through me, like they know what a terrible, out-of-control mess I am behind closed doors. I just... I can't keep living with this kind of shame, I really can't.\n\nDoctor: After these episodes of overeating, do you ever try to compensate for what you've eaten, like making yourself sick, using laxatives, or exercising excessively?\n\nPatient: Oh, heavens no. No, I don't do anything like that. I know... I know you hear about people doing that, young girls especially, making themselves sick on purpose, but I just could never bring myself to do it. The very thought of throwing up just terrifies me, it really does. I haven't thrown up since I had a terrible bout of the stomach flu back in, oh, it must have been 2015, and it was just awful. \n\nAnd laxatives, well, my digestion is already so sensitive at my age, I wouldn't dare take anything like that to clear myself out. I bought some milk of magnesia once years ago, but I never, ever used it for this. I'd be too scared of what it would do to my system. \n\nAnd as for exercise... well, Doctor, look at me. I'm sixty-five years old and my knees certainly aren't what they used to be. The most exercise I get these days is walking around the grocery store or tending to my hydrangeas out front, and even that tires me out if it's too humid outside. I certainly don't go running around the neighborhood trying to burn off all those calories. \n\nSo no, I don't do any of those things. Instead, I just... I just let it sit there. All that food. It just sits right there in my stomach like a great big block of lead. After I finally clean up all those wrappers and crumbs I was telling you about, I usually just end up dragging myself to bed. I have to move so slow because I'm just so uncomfortably full, and I just lay there in the dark. I toss and turn for hours, feeling so bloated and heavy, just feeling every single bite I took weighing me down. It's almost like a punishment, you know? Just having to lay there in bed and feel it all sitting inside me, knowing exactly what I've done to myself and not being able to undo a single bit of it. I just lay there and suffer through the fullness.\n\nDoctor: How often do these episodes of overeating happen, and do they feel like they happen regularly or are they more occasional?\n\nPatient: Oh, they are regular, Doctor. Far too regular, I'm afraid. It really started getting this bad... well, it was after my dear friend passed away. We used to do everything together, you see. We'd talk on the phone every single evening, right around eight o'clock, just catching up on the day or talking about the church bulletin, just little things like that. When she passed... oh, it just left this terrible, quiet hole in my evenings. The house just felt so terribly empty. \n\nAnd that's really when these late-night eating spells took hold of me. Since my friend passed, they've been happening at least once or twice most weeks. Sometimes it feels like even more than that, but definitely once or twice a week, regular as clockwork. \n\nAnd the hardest part is, it just hasn't stopped. I keep telling myself, you know, I sit there and say, 'This is the month I finally get a handle on it. This month I'm going to be better.' I'll even look at my kitchen calendar and pray over it, with all the best intentions in the world. But... there just has not been a month where they really let up. Not a single one. January, February, right through the spring and summer... it just keeps happening. \n\nTuesdays and Thursdays always seem to be the hardest for some reason, maybe because those were the days we used to have our ladies' group, and now I just sit at home missing her. I'll be sitting there in my recliner, trying to watch my programs and keep my mind occupied, but the quiet just creeps in, and before I even realize what I'm doing, I'm back in the pantry again. It's just week after week, month after month of this same awful routine, and I just can't seem to break out of it, no matter how hard I try to wish it away.\n\nDoctor: When you're eating during these late-night episodes, do you feel like you can't stop yourself or control how much you're eating?\n\nPatient: Oh, that is exactly it. That is exactly what it feels like, Doctor. It’s... well, it's honestly the most frightening thing, because I have always prided myself on being a sensible, disciplined person in every other area of my life. But when it comes to this... well, the minute I take that very first bite, especially after the house gets so terribly quiet like I was telling you about, it is just like my good sense completely steps aside. It just walks right out the front door. \n\nI'll be standing there in the kitchen—sometimes I don't even bother turning on the overhead light, just the little light over the stove—and I'll have a box of something, maybe those vanilla wafers or a sleeve of crackers. And right there, loud and clear in my own head, I am telling myself, 'Belinda, stop now. You do not need this, Belinda.' I'm practically begging myself to just fold down the inner wrapper, close up the box, and walk away. \n\nBut it's like my brain and my body aren't even speaking the same language anymore. My hands... they just keep reaching for more. Over and over again. It's like they have a mind entirely of their own, just reaching into the box and grabbing another handful, even while I'm standing there knowing exactly how awful I'm going to feel afterward. I just watch my hands doing it, and I can't seem to make them stop, no matter how hard I'm pleading with myself on the inside. The brakes just go completely out. It’s like I'm a passenger in my own kitchen, just watching myself ruin another evening, and I can't do a single blessed thing to halt it once that first bite happens.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself feeling particularly upset or distressed about your weight or the shape of your body, especially in relation to these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: Well, Doctor, you know... it's funny you ask that, because I know a lot of women my age are always fretting about their figures. You see them on the television, all these commercials for creams and diets and special undergarments, always worrying about a little extra padding here or there. But for me... well, no, not really. I mean, I know I'm not a young woman anymore. I know things shift and settle as we get older, and I've made my peace with that. I really have. \n\nWhen I look in the mirror, I don't sit there and cry over my waistline or the shape of my hips. My mother, rest her soul, she was a larger woman, and she always said a little meat on your bones meant you were healthy and well-fed. So the shape of my body, the number on the scale... that's not what keeps me up at night. It's not what breaks my heart when I'm lying there in the dark after one of my... spells. \n\nWhat distresses me, what really just tears me apart inside, isn't how I look in my clothes the next day. It's the absolute lack of control. It's the feeling that I've let myself down again. It's the fact that I can't seem to stop my own hands from putting food into my mouth, even when my brain is screaming at me to stop. That's what upsets me so terribly. The weight itself, the shape of me... well, that's just a byproduct, isn't it? It's the behavior, the secrecy, the shame of what I'm doing in my own kitchen when nobody is watching. That's the part that hurts. Not the mirror. Just... the knowing what I've done.\n\nDoctor: Do these late-night eating episodes usually happen when you’re feeling particularly sad, lonely, or upset, like after our conversation about your friend passing away?\n\nPatient: Well, you know... I've thought about that a lot, Doctor. I really have. Because it would make so much sense, wouldn't it? If I could just say, 'Oh, I was feeling so blue about Mary passing, and that's why I ate all those cookies.' But... well, it's not quite as simple as that. I mean, yes, the whole thing started after she passed, like I told you, and I certainly miss her every single day. I really do. But these days, when I find myself standing in the pantry... I'm not always crying. I'm not always feeling particularly sad or even thinking about her in that exact moment. \n\nSometimes I'm just... bored, maybe? Or just feeling a little restless. Like last Tuesday, for instance. I had a perfectly fine day. I went to the grocery store, I talked to my niece on the telephone—she's doing so well in her new job, by the way—and I even watched a lovely program on the television about gardens in England. I wasn't upset at all. I was actually feeling quite content. But then eight o'clock rolled around, and the house got quiet, and... well, there I was again. \n\nSo I can't honestly sit here and tell you that I only do it when I'm feeling down in the dumps. It's almost like it's become a habit now, a terrible, terrible habit. It's like my body just expects it, regardless of what my mood is doing. I mean, sure, if I've had a rough day or if I'm feeling particularly lonely, it might trigger it, but... no, I wouldn't say it's just when I'm sad or upset. It happens when I'm perfectly fine, too. And I think that's part of what makes it so confusing to me. If it was just grief, I feel like I could understand it better. But this... this just feels like something else entirely.\n\nDoctor: When you're having one of these late-night eating episodes, do you find yourself eating much more quickly than usual?\n\nPatient: Oh, my heavens, yes. It's... well, it's almost like a race, Doctor, and I don't even know who in the world I'm racing against. I don't even take the time to sit down at the little dinette table. You'd think a person my age would put a portion on a proper plate, maybe get a napkin, and sit down like a civilized human being, but no. I am just standing right there at the kitchen counter. \n\nAnd the speed... well, it's just shameful, really. I eat so fast standing at the kitchen counter that I barely even taste what I'm putting in my mouth. It doesn't even matter which it is, because I'm not enjoying it. I'm just rushing it all down. \n\nI actually... well, sometimes I catch myself swallowing before I've rightly chewed. In the moment, I just can't seem to force myself to slow down and breathe.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the middle of one of these late-night eating episodes, do you feel like you've completely lost control over your eating, like you can't stop even if you wanted to?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. I managed a household for decades, I kept everything running smoothly, I always made sure the bills were paid on time... I was always the one steering the ship, so to speak. But this... this is entirely different. \n\nIt's like... well, once I take that very first bite after the house is quiet—you know, when the television is off and it's just me and the hum of the refrigerator—it's like all my good sense just steps completely aside. It's like there's a total disconnect between my brain and my body. Even while I'm telling myself to stop, my hands just keep reaching for more. I'll just watch my own hand reach out, grab another piece, and bring it to my mouth, and I can't seem to halt the motion no matter how hard I try. It's like being a passenger in my own body, Doctor. I just... I completely lose the reins. And I don't know how to get them back once that first bite happens. I just can't stop it.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually eat these late-night episodes even when you're not actually hungry?\n\nPatient: Hungry? Well, goodness, let me think about that for a moment, Doctor.
